Curve integral formula for the Möbius strip
Amit Suthar

Abstract
The scattering amplitudes for colored scalars can be calculated using the so-called curve integral formula, relying on simple combinatorics. It introduces a set of global Schwinger parameters for all Feynman diagrams that contribute to an amplitude. We extend this construction to non-orientable surfaces by making use of the quasi-cluster algebras defined for non-orientable surfaces. We embed the non-orientable surface in a doubled orientable surface, and project the appropriate features onto the non-orientable surface. The curve integral formula can also be thought of as the high-tension limit of an appropriate string amplitude. As a check of our construction, we take a superstring amplitude with the Möbius strip topology and take its field theory limit to obtain the same Feynman diagrams as in the corresponding curve integral. Our construction can be generalized to arbitrary higher genus non-orientable surfaces. To illustrate this, we list the possible curves and their dual momenta for a two-loop non-orientable surface, and construct the surface Symanzik polynomials using the surface generalization of spanning trees.



1
Introduction

Scattering amplitudes can be expressed as integrals over various moduli spaces. Factorization of the amplitudes follows from the factorization of the moduli spaces. For instance, scattering amplitudes in string theory are integrals of correlation functions of appropriate vertex operators, over the moduli space of Riemann surfaces with locations of vertex operators as marked points
Polchinski:1998rq
;
Polchinski:1998rr
;
Green:1987sp
;
Green:1987mn
. In the field theory limit of string theory scattering amplitudes, the worldsheet moduli space turns into the Schwinger parameters for the Feynman diagrams, i.e., the moduli space of graphs. In more unusual examples, planar
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills amplitudes can been expressed as integrals over Grassmannians
Gr(k,n):
the moduli space of
k
-planes in
n
-dimensions
Arkani-Hamed:2010zjl
;
Arkani-Hamed:2012zlh
; the CHY formula expresses various amplitudes as integrals over the moduli space of the Riemann sphere with marked points
Cachazo:2013hca
;
Cachazo:2013iea
, etc.

The field theory limit of string amplitudes has been studied extensively since the beginning of string theory. For instance, the field theory limit of type-I superstrings gives the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
1
1

1

We refer to the gauge theory with sixteen supercharges as
\mathcal{N}=4
, even though in ten dimensions, there are not four spinors’ worth of supercharges.

Green:1982sw
. In the high string tension limit, one recovers the Feynman diagrams in various regions of the worldsheet moduli space. String theory-inspired methods played a key role in understanding gauge theory and gravity amplitudes. Refer to
Bern:1991an
;
Bern:1992ad
;
Bern:1993sx
;
DiVecchia:1996kf
;
DiVecchia:1996uq
for some of the earlier works. The field theory limit highlights the rich mathematical structure of the degenerations of moduli spaces of Riemann surfaces (for instance, see
Tourkine:2013rda
). However, in all these studies, the integral over the worldsheet moduli space breaks up into a sum of different Feynman diagrams, and there is no sensible single ’moduli space of graphs’ for a QFT amplitude. The
curve integral formula
, discovered in
Arkani-Hamed:2023CurveIntegral
;
Arkani-Hamed:2023Multiplicity
, constructs such a single moduli space of graphs, the
global Schwinger space,
and a QFT amplitude is an integral over it.

Even though the curve integral formula can be thought of as the infinite string tension limit of an appropriate worldsheet string integral, it can be constructed purely combinatorially, without resorting to the worldsheet or vertex operators
Arkani-Hamed:2023CurveIntegral
;
Arkani-Hamed:2023Multiplicity
. It is inspired by the fact that the cluster algebra of surface type, constructed by the triangulations of a surface, can be used to parameterize the Teichmüller space of the surface
fomin2001clusteralgebrasifoundations
;
fock2006modulispaceslocalsystems
. One can quotient out the Teichmüller space of the surface by the Mapping Class Group (MCG) and obtain the moduli space of the surface. Hence, drawing all possible chords or curves on a surface allows one to parameterize the moduli space of the surface and eventually write down an amplitude associated with the surface. See
williams2013clusteralgebrasintroduction
;
fomin2024introductionclusteralgebraschapters
etc. for a mathematical introduction to the subject.

The curve integral formula fits well into the quest of finding ‘alternative methods for scattering amplitudes’. For instance, in the
positive geometry
program, one can find the volumes of certain convex polygons to compute sensible scattering amplitudes. The tree-level amplitude for colored scalars is calculated as the volume of (the dual of) the associahedron constructed in the kinematic space
Arkani-Hamed:2017ABHY
. The moduli space of the tree-level
n
point worldsheet (disk with
n
insertions) is also an associahedron, and the aforementioned associahedron in the kinematic space can be obtained from the worldsheet associahedron by the mapping dictated by the CHY scattering equations
Cachazo:2013hca
;
Cachazo:2013iea
. These investigations acted as a prelude to the curve integral formula
Arkani-Hamed:2019_stringy_canonical_form
;
Arkani-Hamed:2019_binary_geometry
;
Arkani-Hamed:2019vag-AHST
. The subject has been termed
surfaceology,
and connections with NLSM, and YM
Arkani-Hamed:2023-HiddenZeros
;
Arkani-Hamed:2023-ScalarScaffolding
;
Arkani-Hamed:2024nhp
;
Laddha:2024qtn
amplitudes have been discovered.

What is a curve integral formula?

Consider a QFT with matrix-valued field
\phi
, transforming as the adjoint of SU(N), with a
\text{Tr}\phi^{3}
interaction. Feynman diagrams for this theory can be drawn with a double-line notation, and are called ribbon/fat graphs. The perturbation theory can be recast into a sum over topologies of the ribbon graphs. Let
S
be a two-dimensional surface with at least one boundary component, and
n
marked points on the boundaries, and
A_{S}
be the contribution to the
n
-point amplitude with
S
topology of ribbon graphs. The curve integral formula constructs the amplitude
A_{S}
algorithmically using the curves and chords on the surface
S
, as a single integral.

A tree-level trivalent Feynman diagram with
n
legs is dual to a triangulation of an
n
-gon, or a disk with
n
marked points. Each chord/curve connecting two marked points is dual to a propagator of the Feynman diagram, and has a well-defined momentum assigned to it. For an arbitrary order in perturbation theory, a trivalent Feynman diagram is dual to the triangulation of the surface
S
, and every curve
C
has a definite momentum assignment
P_{C}
. Two curves that do not intersect each other are called mutually compatible and can appear together in a Feynman diagram.

Let
E
be the number of internal edges in the trivalent diagrams dual to triangulations of a surface
S
. The Schwinger parameters for a particular Feynman diagram lie in
\mathbb{R}^{E}_{+}
. We can have all the Feynman diagrams obtained from the triangulations of
S
together in the
\mathbb{R}^{E}
space as follows. Let us associate a particular vector
\vec{g}_{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{E}
to every curve
C
on the surface
S
such that the
g
-vectors corresponding to a maximal set of mutually compatible curves make a cone
2
2

2

Given a set of vectors
\{\vec{g}_{i=1,\ldots,k}\}
, a (positive) cone is defined as the following region
\{\vec{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i}\vec{g}_{i}\ |\ c_{i}\geq 0\}
.
in
\mathbb{R}^{E}.
No two cones have an
E
-dimensional intersection, and the union of all cones is the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{E}
. Since a cone is made up of
g
-vectors of a set of
E
mutually compatible curves, it is dual to a complete triangulation
T
of the surface
S
, and corresponds to the Schwinger parameter space of the trivalent graph dual to
T
. For example, let
S
be a disk with five marked points on its boundary. There are five curves on the
S
:
\{C_{13},~C_{14},~C_{24},~C_{25},~C_{35}\}
, and the dual trivalent graphs are five-point tree-level graphs with two propagators. The
g
-vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
are drawn in Figure
1
.
(t_{1},t_{2})
are
global
Schwinger parameters for the five-point tree diagrams.

For every curve
C
, the
headlight function

\alpha_{C}
is defined as the piecewise linear functions dual to the
g
-vectors:
\alpha_{C}(\vec{g}_{C^{\prime}})=\delta_{CC^{\prime}}~.
For instance, for the case of a disk with five insertions, the
g
-vectors given in Figure
1
, and
\alpha_{C}
are as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha_{13}

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)~,

(1)

\displaystyle\alpha_{14}

\displaystyle=-\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)+\text{max}\left(0,t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\alpha_{24}

\displaystyle=-\text{max}\left(0,t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2}\right)+\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)+\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\alpha_{25}

\displaystyle=-t_{1}-\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)+\text{max}\left(0,t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\alpha_{35}

\displaystyle=-t_{2}+\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)~.

Note that in a cone, only the headlight functions dual to the curves making up the cone are non-zero. Following this, we can write the following global Schwinger parameter integral, which evaluates to the sum of all diagrams:

\displaystyle A_{S}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{E}}\mathrm{d}^{E}t\int\mathrm{d}^{d}le^{-\sum_{C}\alpha_{C}P_{C}^{2}}~.

(2)

For a generic surface
S
representing loop processes, we have integration over loop momenta and a non-trivial Mapping Class Group, which needs to be quotiented out. All the ingredients for this integral,
P_{C}~,\alpha_{C}~,\vec{g}_{C}
, can be determined algorithmically using combinatorics of curves on the surface
S
, and is called the
curve integral formula.
We review the algorithms to determine
P_{C}~,\alpha_{C}~,\vec{g}_{C}
very briefly in Appendix
A
. We can integrate out the loop momenta to get a nice form, which we revisit in Section
4
.

g_{14}
g_{13}
g_{25}
g_{35}
g_{24}
t_{1}
t_{2}
\begin{matrix}\alpha_{13}=t_{1}\\
\alpha_{14}=t_{2}\\
\hskip 18.49988pt\alpha_{24},~\alpha_{25},~\alpha_{35}=0\end{matrix}
\begin{matrix}\alpha_{13}=t_{1}\\
\alpha_{35}=-t_{2}\\
\hskip 18.49988pt\alpha_{14}=\alpha_{24}=\alpha_{25}=0\end{matrix}
\begin{matrix}\alpha_{25}=-t_{1}\\
\alpha_{35}=-t_{2}\\
\alpha_{13}=\alpha_{14}=\alpha_{24}=0\end{matrix}
\begin{matrix}\alpha_{24}=t_{2}\\
\alpha_{25}=-t_{1}-t_{2}\\
\alpha_{13}=\alpha_{14}=\alpha_{35}=0\end{matrix}
\begin{matrix}\alpha_{13}=\alpha_{35}=0\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\\
\alpha_{14}=t_{1}+t_{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\\
\alpha_{24}=-t_{1}\\
\hskip 18.49988pt\alpha_{25}=0\end{matrix}
\begin{matrix}\vec{g}_{13}=(1,0)\\
\\
\vec{g}_{14}=(0,1)\\
\\
\vec{g}_{24}=(-1,1)\\
\\
\vec{g}_{25}=(-1,0)\\
\\
\vec{g}_{35}=(0,-1)\end{matrix}

Figure 1:
g
-vectors for
S
being disk with
5
marked points. There are five vectors corresponding to five curves, and the resultant five cones are colored. The headlight functions evaluated in the cones are written.

Color and orientation:

The curve integral formula writes down scattering amplitudes for
\text{Tr}\phi^{3}
scalars, which are equivalent to the adjoint valued
SU(N)
scalars. The matrix-valued field
\phi
carries a fundamental and an anti-fundamental index. We can draw the Feynman diagrams as ribbon graphs to illustrate the color structure. The
SU(N)
fundamental indices dictate that the resulting ribbon graphs be orientable surfaces. Equivalently, only orientable string worldsheets contribute to amplitudes of
SU(N)
Chan-Paton factors. However, in the case of
SO(N)
Chan Paton factors, the state transforming under the adjoint representation carries two anti-symmetric indices, and in the case of
Sp(N)
, two symmetric indices. Since the two indices are on equal footing, one can contract them freely, and we also have non-orientable surfaces. From a purely QFT perspective, if we have
\text{Tr}\phi^{3}
interactions with
\phi
as a symmetric matrix, we have non-orientable surfaces contributing. For an antisymmetric
\phi
, the trivalent term
\phi_{ij}\phi_{jk}\phi_{ki}
vanish, so we can focus on the symmetric matrix
\phi
.

The amplitude for particles that allow for non-orientable surfaces is a sum over orientable and non-orientable ones. Inclusion of non-orientable surfaces results in single-trace contributions at subleading orders in
1/N
.
3
3

3

From the gauge theory amplitude perspective, consider the one-loop box diagram with the color structure
f^{ea_{1}b}f^{ba_{2}c}f^{ca_{3}d}f^{da_{4}e}~.
For
SU(N)
, one can decompose it as follows:

\displaystyle f^{ea_{1}b}f^{ba_{2}c}f^{ca_{3}d}f^{da_{4}e}

\displaystyle=2N\,\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{2}}T^{a_{3}}T^{a_{4}})+2\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{2}})\text{Tr}(T^{a_{3}}T^{a_{4}})

\displaystyle\qquad+2\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{3}})\text{Tr}(T^{a_{2}}T^{a_{4}})+2\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{4}})\text{Tr}(T^{a_{2}}T^{a_{3}})

(3)

For
SO(N)
, we have:

\displaystyle f^{ea_{1}b}f^{ba_{2}c}f^{ca_{3}d}f^{da_{4}e}

\displaystyle=(N-4)\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{2}}T^{a_{3}}T^{a_{4}})-\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{3}}T^{a_{4}}T^{a_{2}})

\displaystyle\qquad-\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{4}}T^{a_{2}}T^{a_{3}})+\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{2}})\text{Tr}(T^{a_{3}}T^{a_{4}})

\displaystyle\qquad+\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{3}})\text{Tr}(T^{a_{2}}T^{a_{4}})+\text{Tr}(T^{a_{1}}T^{a_{4}})\text{Tr}(T^{a_{2}}T^{a_{3}})

(4)

For
SO(N)
, we have single trace contributions at subleading order in
1/N
, which are absent for
SU(N).
Look at
Naculich:2024fiy
;
Bourjaily:2024jbt
;
Bourjaily:2025hvq
for details of color decomposition.
For instance, with a coupling constant
g
, the four-point one-loop amplitude can be expanded as follows:

\displaystyle A_{4}

\displaystyle=\!\!\sum_{\sigma\in\begin{subarray}{c}\text{color}\\
\text{orderings}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\left(g^{2}\,A^{\text{Tree}}_{4}[\sigma]+Ng^{4}\,A_{4}^{\text{Annulus}}[\sigma]+g^{4}\,A_{4}^{\text{M\"{o}bius}}[\sigma]+\mathcal{O}(g^{6})\right)\text{Tr}\big[T_{\sigma(1)}T_{\sigma(2)}T_{\sigma(3)}T_{\sigma(4)}\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+g^{4}\left(\text{multi-trace annulus}\right)+\mathcal{O}(g^{6})~.

(5)

The Möbius strip has a single boundary component and does not contribute to the amplitudes with multi-trace color structures.

Curve integral for non-orientable surfaces:

The usual cluster algebras and the curve integral formula are defined for orientable surfaces. The cluster algebras were generalized to non-orientable surfaces in
dupont2015quasi
. Triangulations of Möbius strips have been studied in
bazier2023number-of-triangulations
. We denote the combinatorial polytope, obtained from the triangulation of Mobius strip with
n
marked points on its boundary as
M_{n}
. It is the analogue of the associahedron
A_{n}
for disk with
n
marked points. One is tempted to ask if these quasi-cluster algebras and
M_{n}
polytope can be used to construct sensible amplitudes. From a stringy perspective, it is expected that the Feynman diagrams appearing in the field theory limit form the same combinatorial structure as the quasi-cluster algebras. We construct the curve integral formula corresponding to the non-orientable surfaces in this article. As a check, we take the field theory limit of the Möbius strip string amplitude and see that we obtain the same diagrams as in the curve integral formula.

A Möbius strip can be represented either by a rectangular strip with two opposite ends identified with a twist, or equivalently as a disk with a cross-cap in the middle. We depict a Möbius strip with two marked points on its boundary in the two representations below:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.57182pt}{41.16463pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{31.64365pt}{41.16463pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

(6)

In the rectangular strip case, the single boundary component appears to be split into an upper and a lower part.

Orientation of the surface is crucial in order to define the necessary ingredients for the curve integral formula. We deal with the non-orientable surfaces by doubling them up into an orientable surface and projecting out the key data from there. For instance, as depicted in (
16
), the Mobius strip with
n
marked points can be doubled into an annulus with
n
marked points on each of the two boundaries.

The curves/chords on the Mobius strip are given by
C_{ij}~,C^{\otimes}_{ij}~,C^{\otimes}~,
see Figure
2
. The curves with superscript
⊗
denote the fact that they passed through the cross-cap. We classify curves on the surface only upto homotopy. So a curve on the Möbius strip passing through the cross-cap twice is equivalent to a curve not passing through the cross-cap. The curve
C^{\otimes}
is a closed curve, and appears only in the tadpoles. The momentum assignment to the curves carrying loop momentum
C_{ij}^{\otimes}
is as follows:

\displaystyle P_{ij}^{\otimes}=l+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{i-1})+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{j-1})~.

(7)

The momentum associated to
C_{ij}
is
P_{ij}=(p_{i}+\ldots+p_{j-1})~.
The kinematic variables are
X_{ik}
and
X_{ik}^{\otimes}
, where
X_{I}=P_{I}^{2}~.

The
g
-vectors and the headlight functions can be calculated using the projection from the doubled orientable surface. Using this, we demonstrate that appropriate projections of the curve integral for orientable surfaces contains the one for non-orientable surfaces as well. The construction can be followed in the same manner for higher genus surfaces as well. We work out the case of a two-loop non-orientable surface.

The field theory limit of appropriate string amplitudes is expected to yield the curve integral formula. A generic loop curve integral formula, being a QFT amplitude, will have bubbles and tadpoles; however, sensible superstring loop amplitudes for massless external states do not have such bubbles and tadpoles. One can start with a bosonic loop open string amplitudes, though they will have some unwanted features. We analyze the well-understood type-I open superstring amplitude with massless external states, and consider its field theory limit. Our objective is to see the emergence of the box diagrams constructed from the quasi-cluster algebras. For a four-particle amplitude, there are eight box diagrams, and we check that different regions of the moduli space integral give the expected eight box diagrams from the quasi-cluster algebras and the curve integral. We identify the regions in the moduli space that would have led to triangles and bubbles, though the integral is zero in those regions.

Outline of the article:

In Section
2
, we review the triangulations of the Mobius strip. In Section
3
, we find the associated momenta to the curves on the Mobius strip, construct the
g
-vectors and headlight functions, and write the curve integral formula. In Section
4
, we construct the surface Symanzik polynomials for the Mobius strip. In Section
5
, we analyze the two-loop non-orientable surface, write down all its curves, their associated momenta, and the surface Symanzik polynomials for the surface. In Section
6
, we study the field theory limit of the Mobius strip superstring amplitude, and check that we obtain the same box diagrams expected from the curve integral.

2
Review of triangulations of the Möbius strip

Traditionally, cluster algebras of surface type have been defined only for orientable surfaces. The natural reason is that we need a handedness on the surface to construct a quiver for a triangulation of the surface. Keeping the curve integral in mind, the turning direction (left/right) of a curve is not defined on a non-orientable surface. The cluster algebras of the surface types were generalized to quasi-cluster algebras for non-orientable surfaces in
dupont2015quasi
. We describe the chords that are used to triangulate a Möbius strip below.

What are the possible chords?

Note that the Möbius strip has one boundary component, and the triangulation and moduli space are well defined for a Möbius strip with at least one marked point on its boundary component. As explained in
dupont2015quasi
;
bazier2023number-of-triangulations
;
bazierquasi-overview
, there are three kinds of chords/curves used for triangulations of a Möbius strip with marked points:

•

C_{ik}^{\otimes}~:~~
The chord
C_{ik}^{\otimes}
joins the marked points
i
and
k
passing through the cross-cap. (Hence the superscript
⊗
in the notation.) While drawing the Möbius strip as a rectangular strip, such curves pass once through the identified vertical lines. Intuitively,
C_{ij}^{\otimes}=C_{ji}^{\otimes}~.
With
n
marked points, there are
n(n+1)/2
such chords.

(8)

•

C_{ik}~:~~

C_{ik}
are the same chords we use in the case of triangulations of an annulus. A chord
C_{ik}
joins the marked points
i
and
k
passing around the cross-cap
4
4

4

In the case of an annulus, cross-cap gets replaced by the hole (second boundary component) of the annulus.
. Since there are two ways around the cross-cap,
C_{ik}
and
C_{ki}
are distinct. While drawing the Möbius strip as a rectangular strip, such curves must either pass through the identified vertical lines twice or not at all. There are
n(n-1)
such curves, following the same counting as in the case of an annulus.

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}i\in[1,n]~,\ \ j\in[i+2,i+n]~;~~~{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}C_{ij}}~:\\

(9)

•

C^{\otimes}~:~~
There is a single chord
C^{\otimes}
that cuts the Möbius strip
in the middle .

5
5

5

Upon cutting a Möbius strip along its length, instead of obtaining two disconnected pieces, we obtain a single orientable surface of double the length of the original Möbius strip with two twists. The surface obtained after the cut is topologically an annulus. We should revisit it afterwards.
Inclusion of this curve is necessary to obtain a consistent combinatorial structure.

Figure 2:
List of all the curves on the Möbius strip with two marked points.

For a Möbius strip with a single marked point, there are two chords possible:
C^{\otimes}
and
C^{\otimes}_{11}~,
as depicted in Figure
3
.

Which chords are compatible?

Two curves are compatible with each other if they can be drawn on the surface without intersections. The cross-cap region makes the compatibility of curves opaque. The rectangular strip picture makes it comparatively clearer. Note that a cross-cap is defined by identifying the anti-podal points of a region, so
any curve entering a cross-cap must exit from the anti-podal point.
We can end up with incompatible curves intersecting nowhere away from the cross-cap, as in (
12
), if we don’t draw curves passing through the cross-caps consistently. Note that multiple compatible curves can pass through the cross-cap as follows:

✗

\otimes

\equiv

\otimes

✓

\otimes

✓

\otimes

We depict the compatibility of curves using two examples below for Mobius strip with
n=2
:

•

C^{\otimes}_{12}
is compatible with
C_{11}^{\otimes}
and
C_{22}^{\otimes}
, but not with
C_{11}
and
C_{22}
:

(11)

We have colored the two triangles obtained by triangulation using
C_{12}^{\otimes}
and
C_{11}^{\otimes}
in the left picture. For the right picture, one can convince oneself that there is no way to draw
C_{11}
and
C_{12}^{\otimes}
on a rectangular strip without them crossing.

•

Even though one can draw
C_{12}^{\otimes}
and
C^{\otimes}
such that they do not intersect anywhere apart from the cross-cap, they are not compatible:

(12)

How to define quasi-mutations and the combinatorial polytope?

To define the quasi-cluster algebra, we need to generalize the idea of mutations to the chords on unorientable surfaces.

Instead of describing the detailed rules of such quasi-mutations, we write down the examples of the combinatorial polytopes
M_{1}
and
M_{2}~,
obtained from quasi-triangulations of Mobius strip with
1
and
2
marked points respectively.
M_{1,2}
are shown in Figure
3
. Apart from the usual mutations we encounter in the orientable surface triangulations, the new addition is the
quasi-mutations
of
C_{11}^{\otimes}\longleftrightarrow C^{\otimes}~.
In the next subsection, we should see that these quasi-mutations can be naturally understood as obtained from the projection of triangulations of an orientable surface.

Let us add up the total number of chords possible
C_{ij}^{\otimes}~,~C_{ij}~,~C^{\otimes}
for Möbius strip with
n
insertions.

\displaystyle\text{Number of chords on M\"{o}bius}_{n}=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}+n(n-1)+1=\frac{3n^{2}-n+2}{2}~.

(13)

This is the number of codimension-one faces of the combinatorial polytope
M_{n}
. The total number of vertices of this
M_{n}
polytope has been counted in
bazier2023number-of-triangulations
to be the following:

\displaystyle\text{Number of total triangulations of M\"{o}bius}_{n}=4^{n-1}+{}^{2n-2}C_{n-1}~.

(14)

Let us compare it with the planar one-loop (
n
marked points on one boundary of the annulus, and none on the other) case of
\hat{D}_{n}:

Laddha:2024qtn

\displaystyle\text{Number of total triangulations of Annulus}_{n}=2(2n-1)\,C_{n-1}=\frac{2(2n-1)!}{n!\,(n-1)!}~.

(15)

Note that
C_{n}
is the Catalan number. The total number of triangulations for
n=1
and
n=2
is the same for the annulus and the Möbius strip. For
n=3
, there are a total of 22 triangulations for the Möbius strip and 20 for the annulus. There are two more triangulations in the Möbius strip dual to the triangle graphs.

C^{\otimes}_{11}:~
\otimes
\times
1
\cong
\times
1
M_{1}
C^{\otimes}:~
\otimes
\times
1
\cong
\times
1
M_{2}
\otimes
\times
1
\times
2
\otimes
\times
\times
\otimes
\times
1
\times
2
\otimes
\times
\times

\times
1
\times
2
\otimes
\times
\times
\otimes
\times

\times

\otimes

\times

\times
\times

\times
1
\times

Figure 3:
Combinatorial polytopes
M_{1,2}
obtained from (quasi-)mutations of the Möbius strip with one and two marked points.

3
Curve integral for Möbius strip

In this section, we use the mathematical structures built on the triangulations of the Möbius strip to study the scattering amplitudes defined as integrals over the moduli space of non-orientable surfaces with marked points. We wish to mimic the curve integral formula studied in
Arkani-Hamed:2023CurveIntegral
;
Arkani-Hamed:2023Multiplicity
. We need to associate a momentum, a
g
-vector, and eventually a headlight function to every chord/curve on the Möbius strip. Since these structures are well understood for orientable surfaces, we embed our Möbius strip in an orientable surface, an annulus, and project out the required information from the orientable surface.

Doubling the Möbius strip into an annulus:

We wish to embed the unorientable surface into a bigger orientable surface. We proceed as depicted in (
16
). We begin with the Möbius strip, take its mirror image, and glue the mirror image to the original surface. As also discussed in
bazierquasi-overview
, in terms of the disk with a cross-cap, the doubling amounts to blowing up the cross-cap into a boundary and putting identical marked points on the new boundary. The ordering of the marked points on the inside boundary is also clockwise, analogous to the original ones.

(16)

Calculating the
g
-vectors:

Without the doubling of the Möbius strip and the embedding into an orientable surface, it is not clear how to define
g
-vectors for the curves on the Möbius strip. In the absence of an orientation, it is meaningless to say that the curve took a left or a right turn.

Along with the doubling of the Möbius strip, a curve on the Möbius strip gets doubled into a set of two curves. We depict this using the example of
C_{12}^{\otimes}
for the case of Möbius
2
in (
17
). The curve has been mapped to
C_{12^{\prime}}
and
C_{21^{\prime}}
on the annulus obtained from the doubling. It is a general feature: apart from
C^{\otimes}
, every curve on the Möbius strip maps to two distinct curves on the doubled annulus.

(17)

The
g
-vector fan of the Möbius strip should be obtained from an appropriate projection of the
g
-vector fan of the doubled annulus with double the marked points. The projection is determined as follows:
the
g
-vectors of the two curves on the annulus, mapping to the same curve on the Möbius strip, should become equal under the projection.
We quantify this projection by working out some examples. By inspection, we deduce the appropriate projection to be the following:

\displaystyle t_{i}+t_{i}^{\prime}=0~,\qquad\forall\quad t_{i}~.

(18)

It projects out an
n
-dimensional space in the
2n
-dimensional space in which the
g
-vectors for the doubled annulus reside. The statement is independent of the reference triangulation. For a generic reference triangulation of the doubled annulus with coordinates
\{t_{i},t_{i}^{\prime}\}~,
where
t_{i}^{\prime}
and
t_{i}
are parameters for the two edges related to each other by a flip of two boundaries. For detailed worked examples, refer to Figures
5
and
17
for
n=1
and
n=2
, respectively. Finally, Figure
4
depicts the resultant
g
-vector fan for the two cases.

The
g
-vectors for the Möbius strip are defined for a reference triangulation specified by the annulus reference triangulation. Here, in Figures
5
,
17
and
4
, the reference triangulation is the analogue of the ’wheel-graph’: the
n
-gon.

Momentum assignment to the curves:

n=2

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

(19)

We associate momenta with the curves on the Möbius strip. The usual rule to associate momentum with a curve on an orientable surface uses handedness and can not be used for a Möbius strip. We obtain the momentum by resorting to the doubled annulus. A curve on the Möbius strip gets mapped to two curves on the doubled annulus. Initially, we let the doubled insertions on the annulus have arbitrary momenta
\{p_{i}^{\prime}\}
, different from the original momenta
\{p_{i}\}
on the Möbius strip. We determine the momenta
\{p_{i}^{\prime}\}
in terms of
\{p_{i}\}
by demanding that the momenta of the two doubled curves should match. To concretize the proposal, and keeping in mind the expected
telescopic property
of the curve integral, we draw a tadpole graph with explicit momenta assignments in Figure
6
, and double it to obtain the annulus. Note that the momentum associated with a curve does not depend on any reference triangulation. We have made an odd choice for loop momenta,
l-p_{3}
, for later convenience. We can draw all the curves and their doubled ones on the annulus, calculate the momenta, and find an appropriate projection on the momenta as follows:

\displaystyle p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i-1}~.

(20)

It should be understood that
p_{1}^{\prime}=p_{n}~.
Analogous to the doubling of a tadpole graph for
n=3
in Figure
6
, we depict the doubled annulus obtained from the doubling of a tadpole graph of a Möbius strip with an arbitrary number of marked points in Figure
7
. Calculating the momenta for doubled curves, and using the identification
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i-1}
, we obtain the following:

\displaystyle P_{ij}^{\otimes}=l+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{i-1})+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{j-1})~.

(21)

P_{ij}^{\otimes}
is symmetric in
i
and
j
, as it should be. Had we chosen the loop momentum on the tadpole leg as
l
rather than
l-p_{n}
, we would have obtained
P_{ij}^{\otimes}=l+p_{n}+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{i-1})+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{j-1})~.

C_{ij}^{\otimes}
(and
C^{\otimes}
) are the only curves carrying loop momenta. While dealing with the one-loop kinematic space in the planar case, we usually double up the external momenta and impose the identification at the end, as done in
mrunmay2304
;
mrunmay2206
, etc. The doubling of the kinematic variables is distinct from the doubling of non-orientable surfaces onto the orientable ones, discussed in this section. The kinematic space constructed here:
\{X_{ik},~X_{ik}^{\otimes}\}
needs to be doubled further, along the lines of
mrunmay2304
;
mrunmay2206
, etc., to have a kinematic space suitable for tadpoles and bubbles on external legs. Though we do not pursue this in this manuscript.

Figure 6:
A particular Feynman diagram for Möbius
3
, a tadpole, doubled to obtain an annulus with 6 insertions. The curve
C^{\otimes}_{23}
gets doubled to
C_{2^{\prime}3}
and
C_{23^{\prime}}
on the annulus.

p_{1}
p_{2}
p_{i}
p_{j}
p_{n}
p_{1}^{\prime}
p_{2}^{\prime}
p_{i}^{\prime}
p_{j}^{\prime}
p_{n}^{\prime}
(l-p_{n})
(l-p_{n})

Figure 7:
Finding the momenta of the curve
C_{ij}^{\otimes}:
The momenta of the doubled curves on the annulus are
P_{ji^{\prime}}=(p_{1}^{\prime}+\ldots+p_{i}^{\prime})+l-p_{n}+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{j-1})
and
P_{ij^{\prime}}=(p_{1}^{\prime}+\ldots+p_{j}^{\prime})+l-p_{n}+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{i-1})
. Under the identification
p_{i}^{\prime}=p_{i-1}
, we obtain
P_{ji^{\prime}}
,
P_{ij^{\prime}}

\to P_{ij}^{\otimes}=l+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{i-1})+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{j-1})
.

Let us perform a cross-check on our momenta assignments to the curves
C_{ik}^{\otimes}~.
Let us choose
n=3
, and draw a complete triangulation corresponding to a triangle graph, as shown in Figure
16
. Let us choose a momenta
P_{11}^{\otimes}\equiv l
as the definition of our
l~.
This fixes momentum assignments to other curves in the same triangulation. Mutating once gets us to another triangulation with a single different curve. Momentum conservation in the new triangulation gives us the momentum assignment for the new curve in the new triangulation. Following the pattern, we can find all the
P_{ik}^{\otimes}:

\displaystyle P_{11}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=l~,\quad

\displaystyle\quad P_{12}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=l+p_{1}~,

(22)

\displaystyle P_{22}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=l+2p_{1}~,\quad

\displaystyle\quad P_{23}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=l+p_{1}-p_{3}~,

\displaystyle P_{33}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=l-2p_{3}~,\quad

\displaystyle\quad P_{31}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=l-p_{3}~.

It agrees with our earlier answer (
21
).

The momenta associated with the curves
C_{ij}
are the same as the planar one-loop case. Since these curves steer clear of the cross-cap, even if we replace the cross-cap with another boundary component, they remain unaffected, and their momenta also remain the same. So, we have:

\displaystyle P_{ij}=p_{i}+p_{i+1}+\ldots+p_{j-1}~.

(23)

The curve
C^{\otimes}
appears in the tadpole as the loop leg, hence the associated momentum
P^{\otimes}
should carry loop momentum.
P^{\otimes}
does not carry any particle label, and hence it must be independent of any external particle label. So, it should be
P^{\otimes}\propto l~.
It is not possible to determine this momentum via mutations, as done in Figure
16
. Let us reiterate that these quasi-triangulations with
C^{\otimes}
, all of them being tadpoles, behave in a non-standard way, and we will find a consistent way to avoid these.

Headlight functions

We have embedded the Möbius strip with
n
marked points on its boundary into an annulus with
n
marked points on either of its boundaries. With the ’wheel’ graph as the reference triangulation, we have realized how the global Schwinger parameters for the Möbius strip are embedded into the global Schwinger parameters for the doubled annulus:
t_{i}^{\prime}+t_{i}=0~.
We can go ahead and write down the headlight functions
\alpha_{C}
for the doubled annulus and project them onto the
t_{i}^{\prime}+t_{i}=0
space to obtain the headlight functions for the curves on the Möbius strip. For the
g
-vectors calculated with respect to the wheel graph as the reference triangulation in Figure
4
, we calculate the headlight functions to obtain the following:

\displaystyle\text{For }n=1:\qquad\vec{g}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=-1~,

\displaystyle\quad\vec{g}^{\otimes}_{11}

\displaystyle=+1~;

(24)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\otimes}(t)

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(0,t\right)-t~,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\otimes}_{11}(t)

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(0,t\right)~.

For
n=2
, we have:

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{11}

\displaystyle=(1,-1)~~,\hskip 56.9055pt\vec{g}_{22}=(-1,-1)~~,

(25)

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{12}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=(0,1)~~,\hskip 65.44142pt\vec{g}^{\otimes}_{11}=(1,0)~~,

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{22}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=(-1,0)~~,\hskip 56.9055pt\vec{g}^{\otimes}=(0,-1)~.

\displaystyle\alpha_{11}

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)+2\,\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)-\text{max}\left(0,\,t_{1},\,t_{2}+2\,\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)\right)~,

(26)

\displaystyle\alpha_{22}

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)+2\,\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)-\text{max}\left(t_{2},\,t_{1}+\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)+\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)\right)~,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\otimes}_{12}

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\otimes}_{11}

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(0,\,t_{2},\,t_{1}+t_{2}\right)-\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\otimes}_{22}

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(t_{1},\,t_{2},\,t_{1}+t_{2}\right)-t_{1}-\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)~,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\text{max}\left(t_{1},\,t_{2}+\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)+2\,\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)\right)-t_{2}-\text{max}\left(0,t_{2}\right)-2\,\text{max}\left(0,t_{1}\right)~.

We write down the explicit headlight functions for
n=3
obtained similarly, in the Appendix (
106
).

We have calculated the momenta associated with the curves and their corresponding
g
-vectors and headlight functions. There is no non-trivial Mapping Class Group for the Möbius strip. With this much data, we can write down the curve integral formula:

\displaystyle A_{n}^{\text{M\"{o}bius}}=\int\mathrm{d}^{n}t_{i}\int\mathrm{d}^{D}l\,e^{-\sum_{C}\alpha_{C}X_{C}}~.

(27)

We carry out the explicit loop integration in the Appendix (
97
) - (
104
) , and express the curve integral in terms of the surface Symanzik polynomials. We discuss this further in Section
4
, where we construct these surface Symanzik polynomials from
spanning subsurfaces
.

Telescopic property

Let us perform the analysis of calculating the
g
-vectors and the headlight functions with a different reference triangulation, namely a tadpole depicted in Figure
7
. It will help us understand the properties of the curve integral that are common to all
n
. Figure
8
depicts the Schwinger parameter labels on the doubled annulus graph.

1
2
i
j
n
t_{1}
t_{i-1}
t_{n-1}
1^{\prime}
2^{\prime}
i^{\prime}
j^{\prime}
n^{\prime}
t_{1}^{\prime}
t_{i-1}^{\prime}
t_{n-1}^{\prime}
t_{0}
t_{0}^{\prime}

Figure 8:
The Schwinger parameters on the doubled annulus and the two curves obtained from doubling of
C_{ij}^{\otimes}
are shown.

We employ the same rule that the
g
-vectors of the two curves on the doubled annulus should map to the same vector under the projection. The same projection
t_{i}^{\prime}+t_{i}=0~,\ \ \forall\ i\in[0,(n-1)]
works. To demonstrate it, following Figure
8
, let us find the
g
-vector of
C_{ij}^{\otimes}
(
i,j\neq 1,n
) for generic
n
:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\vec{g}^{\text{aux}}_{i^{\prime}j}=\big(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{0}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{j-1}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{n-1}}}{{-1}}~;~\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t^{\prime}_{i-1}}}{{-1}},\ldots\big)}\\
\quad\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\vec{g}^{\text{aux}}_{ij^{\prime}}=\big(\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i-1}}}{{1}},\ldots~:~\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t^{\prime}_{0}}}{{-1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t^{\prime}_{j-1}}}{{-1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t^{\prime}_{n-1}}}{{1}}\big)}\end{matrix}\ \Rightarrow\ \begin{matrix}\vec{g}_{ij}^{\otimes}=\big(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{0}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i-1}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{j-1}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{n-1}}}{{-1}}\big)\\
{}_{(i,j\neq 1,n)}\end{matrix}~.

(28)

We have written only the non-zero components of the vectors, and all the omitted ones, denoted as
\ldots
, are zero. For
i,j
taking the values
1
or
n
, the
g
-vectors are changed to be the following:

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{11}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\big(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{0}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{n-1}}}{{-1}}\big)~,

(29)

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{1j}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\big(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{0}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{j-1}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{n-1}}}{{-1}}\big)\qquad j\in[2,\ldots,n-1]~,

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{1n}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\big(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{0}}}{{1}},\ldots\big)~,

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{in}^{\otimes}

\displaystyle=\big(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{0}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i-1}}}{{1}},\ldots\big)\quad\qquad i\in[2,\ldots,n]~.

The curves
C_{ij}
(and the doubled curves on the annulus) never take any turn at
t_{0}
(and
t_{0}^{\prime}
). They trace out the same path as the curves
C_{ij}
in the case of the planar one-loop case, and thus have the same
g
-vectors:

\displaystyle\text{For }k\in[3,n+1]~:\qquad\vec{g}_{1k}

\displaystyle=\big(\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{k-2}}}{{-1}},\ldots\big)~,

\displaystyle\text{For }i\in[2,n-1]~,~~k\in[i+2,n+1]~:\qquad\vec{g}_{ik}

\displaystyle=\big(\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i-1}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{k-2}}}{{-1}},\ldots\big)~,

\displaystyle\text{For }i\in[2,n-1]~,~~k\in[n+2,n+i]~:\qquad\vec{g}_{ik}

\displaystyle=\big(\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i-1}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{j-1}}}{{1}},\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{n-1}}}{{-1}},\ldots\big)~,

\displaystyle\text{For }k\in[2,n]~:\qquad\vec{g}_{nk}

\displaystyle=\big(\ldots,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{k-1}}}{{1}},\ldots\big)~.

(30)

Note that the indices
i,k
are defined modulo
n
, so that
C_{1(n+1)}\equiv C_{11}~.
Finally, inspecting the closed
\Delta
curve in the doubled annulus, we find the
g
-vector for the
C^{\otimes}
:

\displaystyle\vec{g}^{\otimes}=\big(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{0}}}{{-1}},\ldots\big)~.

(31)

We draw the
g
-vector fan for
n=1
and
n=2
in Figure
9
and write down the
g
-vectors for
n=3
in the Appendix.

Figure 9:
g
-vector fans for the Möbius strip with one or two marked points on its boundary, with a tadpole graph as the reference triangulation.

Since the curves on the Möbius strip and the doubled annulus are going to be ’tree-curves’ attached to the tadpole, the curve matrices and hence the headlight functions can be evaluated using the information of tree-curves.
All the curves not carrying any loop momentum, namely the
C_{ij}
curves, lie on a co-dimension one plane:
t_{0}=0~.
It is a common feature in the annulus and Möbius strip. However, the
g
-vectors for
C_{ij}
curves do not lie on a co-dimension one space for the reference triangulation ’wheel’ graph
4
. The tadpole reference triangulation simplifies the surface Symanzik polynomial:
\mathcal{U}=t_{0}
, as we shall see in the next section. Also, the only curve with
t_{0}<0
is
C^{\otimes}
for all
n~.

We restrict ourselves to the
t_{0}>0
region to avoid tadpoles arising from quasi-triangulations.

4
Constructing surface Symanzik polynomials

In this section, we briefly review the construction of surface Symanzik polynomials from simple topological considerations, and repeat the same for the Mobius strip.

Definition of surface Symanzik polynomials:

A generic curve integral for
n
external particles, associated with a surface
S
, prior to the loop momenta integration is as follows:

\displaystyle A_{n}^{S}=\int\bigwedge\mathrm{d}^{E}t_{i}\,\mathcal{K}(t_{i})\int\prod_{i}\mathrm{d}^{D}l_{i}\,e^{-\sum_{C}\alpha_{C}X_{C}}~,

(32)

where
E
is the dimension of the Schwinger space (number of internal edges),
D
is the number of spacetime dimensions, and
\mathcal{K}(t_{i})
is the MCG fixing kernel. One can carry out the loop momenta to obtain the following form of the curve integral:

\displaystyle A_{n}^{S}=\int\bigwedge\mathrm{d}^{E}t_{i}\,\mathcal{K}(t_{i})\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{U}_{S}^{D/2}}e^{-\mathcal{F}_{S}/\mathcal{U}_{S}-\mathcal{Z}_{S}}~.

(33)

\mathcal{U}_{S},~\mathcal{F}_{S},~\mathcal{Z}_{S}
are called the surface Symanzik polynomials.
\mathcal{U}_{S}
and
\mathcal{F}_{S}
are the global Schwinger generalizations of Symanzik polynomials for a particular graph.

Symanzik polynomials for a graph:

Symanzik polynomials
U_{\gamma}
and
F_{\gamma}
for a particular graph
\gamma
can be constructed by simple topological considerations. Let
\{s_{i}\}
be the set of Schwinger parameters for the internal edges of
\gamma
. Spanning-1 trees are defined to be the tree subgraphs
\gamma_{1}\subset\gamma
obtained from the minimal deletion of edges. For instance, for a four-point box diagram, there are four spanning-1 trees obtained from deleting either one of the four loop legs. Similarly, a spanning-2 tree is defined to be a disjoint sum of two tree graphs
\gamma_{1}\sqcup\gamma_{2}\subset\gamma
obtained from minimal deletion of edges. We have the following:

\displaystyle U_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma_{1}\in\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spanning-}1\\
\text{trees}\end{subarray}\right\}}\prod_{s_{i}\in\gamma/\gamma_{1}}s_{i}~,

(34)

\displaystyle F_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma_{1}\sqcup\gamma_{2}\in\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spanning-}2\\
\text{trees}\end{subarray}\right\}}p_{\gamma_{1}}.p_{\gamma_{2}}\prod_{s_{i}\in\gamma/(\gamma_{1}\sqcup\gamma_{2})}s_{i}~.

p_{\gamma_{1(2)}}
is the sum of external momenta flowing in the subgraph
\gamma_{1(2)}~.
These remarkable properties let us write down the Schwinger integrand for a given loop diagram without doing the actual loop integration. Refer to
weinzierl
;
smirnovbook1
;
smirnovbook2
for more details.

Surface Symanzik polynomials determined from spanning-surfaces

Analogous to the Symanzik polynomials for a particular graph, the surface Symanzik polynomials can also be written in terms of surface generalizations of spanning trees
Arkani-Hamed:2023CurveIntegral
;
Arkani-Hamed:2023Multiplicity
. We review it for the orientable surfaces with the examples of planar one and two-loop
n
-point amplitudes, and then use it for non-orientable surfaces. The examples also show the efficiency of writing down higher loop global Schwinger
integrands
for the full amplitude.

Given a surface
S
, we have

•

A spanning-1 surface
s_{1}
is defined to be a subsurface of
S
, with the inclusion of a maximal set of mutually compatible curves such that the curves do not separate
S
into two disjoint pieces.

•

A spanning-2 surface
s_{1}\sqcup s_{2}
is defined by an arbitrary intersection of two spanning-1 surfaces, such that
S
is separated precisely into two disjoint pieces:
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
.

As a trivial example, for a disk with
n
marked points on its boundary, all the curves separate it into two disjoint pieces, hence no spanning-1 surfaces. Here are the spanning surfaces for
S
being an annulus with
n
marked points on its boundary
6
6

6

To keep the analysis simple, we are restricting ourselves to half of the Schwinger region such that there is a single curve starting from an insertion and going around the puncture.
:

\displaystyle\text{spanning-1 surfaces:}\quad\left\{\ \ \hbox to43.66pt{\vbox to48.04pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.82733pt\lower-21.49295pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The surface Symanzik polynomials in terms of the spanning surfaces are given as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1}\in\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spanning-}1\\
\text{surfaces}\end{subarray}\right\}}\prod_{C_{i}\in S/s_{1}}\alpha_{C_{i}}~,

(37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{S}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1}\sqcup s_{2}\in\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spanning-}2\\
\text{surfaces}\end{subarray}\right\}}p_{s_{1}}.p_{s_{2}}\prod_{C_{i}\in S/(s_{1}\sqcup s_{2})}\alpha_{C_{i}}~.

(38)

Using these definitions, we can readily write down the
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{F}
for any surface without actually carrying out the loop integral. In both cases, for spanning-2 surfaces, the disjoint surfaces have incoming momenta in such a way that
p_{s_{1}}.p_{s_{2}}=X_{ij}~,
the usual planar kinematic variable. Hence, for one-loop, we have
8
8

8

We have restricted ourselves to on-shell external particles, hence
n>3~,
and
X_{ii}=X_{i,i+1}=0
.
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\text{planar},L=1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{C_{i}^{\prime}}~,\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\text{planar},L=1}=\sum_{i+1<j}X_{ij}\,\alpha_{C_{i}^{\prime}}\alpha_{C_{j}^{\prime}}~.

(39)

For the two-loop, let us denote the two punctures as
a,b
, and we have (terms arranged as in (
36
)):

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\text{pl},L=2}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{ab}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{C_{ai}}+\alpha_{ab}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{C_{bi}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{C_{ai}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{C_{bi}}~,

(40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{pl},L=2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<j}X_{ij}\left(\sum_{k}\alpha_{ai}\alpha_{aj}\alpha_{bk}+\sum_{k}\alpha_{bi}\alpha_{bj}\alpha_{ak}+\alpha_{bi}\alpha_{bj}\alpha_{ab}+\alpha_{ai}\alpha_{aj}\alpha_{ab}+\alpha_{ai}\alpha_{bj}\alpha_{ab}\right)

In both cases,
\mathcal{Z}
contains the terms from curves with momenta independent of the loop momentum:
\mathcal{Z}=\sum_{i<j-1}\alpha_{ij}X_{ij}~.
The whole Schwinger integrand is determined, and the external momenta appear only as
X_{ij}~.

(41)

The curves
C^{\otimes}_{ik}
cut open a Möbius strip into a disk as shown in Figure
10
. Since
C_{ij}^{\otimes}
is symmetric in its two indices, so to avoid double counting, we have
i\leq j~.

Note that
C^{\otimes}
curve on a Möbius strip also does not separate it into two disjoint pieces, as shown in Figure
12
. It is a reflection of the fact that the momentum associated with
C^{\otimes}
is the loop momentum. Famously, cutting the Möbius strip along its length does not divide it into two parts, but rather turns it into an orientable surface, topologically a cylinder. To include the possibility of such curves, we defined our spanning-1 surfaces as subsurfaces with the inclusion of
non-separating curves
, so that the resultant subsurface is still a single connected piece, not necessarily a disk. However, we restrict to the region in the global Schwinger space
t_{0}<0
(with the tadpole (Figure
8
) as the reference triangulation), so that the headlight
\alpha^{\otimes}
is
turned off
for all
n
. We omit the quasi-triangulations coming from
C^{\otimes}
, and as a result, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\text{M\"{o}b}}=\sum_{i\leq k}\alpha^{\otimes}_{ik}~.

(42)

The spanning-2 surfaces for the Möbius strip are made up of two curves
C^{\otimes}_{ij}
and
C^{\otimes}_{kl}~.

\displaystyle\text{spanning-2 surfaces:}\ \ \left\{\ \hbox to53.7pt{\vbox to56.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 26.26286pt\lower-28.94241pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(43)

Figure
11
depicts how the two curves breaks open the Möbius strip into two disks. By convention
i\leq j
and
k\leq l
, and to avoid double counting, we choose
i\leq k~.
With these constraints, the two curves
C_{ij}^{\otimes}
and
C_{kl}^{\otimes}
are compatible only if
i\leq k\leq j\leq l~.
The momentum flowing inside the two disks, as shown in Figure
11
is
(P_{kj}+P_{li})~.
Note that
P_{ik}\equiv p_{i}+p_{i+1}+\ldots+p_{k-1}~,
and hence
P_{ik}+P_{kj}+P_{jl}+P_{li}=0~.
Hence, the second Symanzik polynomial is as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{Mob}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\leq k\leq j\leq l}\alpha_{ij}^{\otimes}\alpha_{kl}^{\otimes}(P_{kj}+P_{li})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\leq k\leq j\leq l}\alpha_{ij}^{\otimes}\alpha_{kl}^{\otimes}\big(X_{ik}+X_{jl}+X_{il}+X_{kj}-X_{ij}-X_{kl}\big)~.

(44)

The following identities might be useful to obtain the last equality:
P_{ik}=P_{1k}-P_{1i}~,~~2P_{1i}.P_{1k}=X_{1i}+X_{1k}-X_{ik}~.
The surface Symanzik polynomials obtained from the spanning surfaces match with the explicit momentum integral carried out in the Appendix
B
: (
97
) - (
104
) .

5
Two-loop non-orientable surface

In this subsection, we briefly discuss how the constructions for non-orientable surfaces generalize to the higher genus surfaces. With a cross-cap and a puncture, we can construct higher genus surfaces. At two loops, along with the orientable surface, a disk with two punctures, we receive a contribution subdominant as

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(45)

The surface
S_{(2)}
is obtained from cutting out a small disk from the Möbius strip. We shall discuss the curves and triangulations of this surface below.

What about a surface with two cross-caps? A sphere with two cross-caps is a Klein bottle, and a disk with two cross-caps is a Klein bottle with a disk removed. The
handle
due to the Klein bottle represents the propagation of an uncolored particle, a closed string propagation. However, in our current model, we do not have uncolored particles, and the
\Phi
excitations can not propagate in the Klein bottle. So, in the current setup, a disk with cross-caps does not contribute due to the same reason that a 2-torus with a disk removed does not contribute. One can imagine a different setup with uncolored particles, and such surfaces will contribute. For instance:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\supset-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\mu}\sigma)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}(\partial\Phi.\partial\Phi)-\frac{1}{3}\text{Tr}(\Phi^{3})-\frac{1}{3}\sigma\text{Tr}(\Phi^{2})~.

(46)

We do not discuss such cases with uncolored fields.

The curves and their momenta:

The surface
S_{(2)}
will have the following possible curves:

\displaystyle C_{0i}:

The surface
S^{(2)}
has a non-trivial Mapping Class Group (MCG) due to the Dehn-twist:
\otimes
\times
\times

The resulting quasi-cluster algebra and hence the Teichmüller space is infinite-dimensional. There are infinitely many distinct copies of curves
C_{0i}~,C_{ik}^{\otimes}~,C_{0i}
and
C_{ik}
based on how they wind around the puncture and cross-cap. There are also three copies of
C_{ik}:

\otimes
\times
\times
. These carry the same momenta, and while specifying a particular triangulation, we need to specify the particular curve. We omit this difference to keep the notations clean. So, each of these curves carries an extra index, with the information of windings and their path, suppressed here. The combinatorial polytope obtained from the mutations never closes onto itself and is an infinite structure. Analogous to the Faddeev-Popov gauge fixing, one can fix the MCG by inserting a
Mirzakhani kernel
(equivalent to the factor of
1/(\text{Vol MCG})
): an appropriate set of headlight functions, in the curve integral. It picks up a finite region in the Teichmüller space, and gives us the moduli space of the surface.

The momentum assignment to a curve is the same for all its MCG variants. The momentum assignment to the following curves is straightforward:

\displaystyle C_{ij}\leftrightarrow P_{ij}=p_{i}+\ldots+p_{j-1}~,\qquad C_{0i}\leftrightarrow P_{0i}=l_{1}+P_{1i}~,\qquad C_{ik}^{\otimes}\leftrightarrow P_{ik}^{\otimes}=l_{2}+P_{1i}+P_{1k}~.

(48)

We avoid the cumbersome procedure of drawing the doubled orientable surface, and determine the momentum for some of the curves by looking at subsurfaces as follows:

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\ \ \ \begin{matrix}\Rightarrow\ \ P^{\otimes}_{00}=l_{2}+2(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{i-1}-P_{0i})\\
\quad\\
\Rightarrow\ \ P^{\otimes}_{00}=l_{2}-2l_{1}~.\end{matrix}

(50)

We are left with three curves:
C^{\otimes}~,C^{\otimes}_{(0)}
and
C_{00(\otimes)}~.
Analogous to the Möbius strip case,
C^{\otimes}
and
C_{(0)}^{\otimes}
are the tadpole loop curves. In the string moduli space, they correspond to a (unoriented) closed string insertion (
\tau\to 0
). Just like the Möbius strip case, we choose not to include such degeneration and graphs. The last curve,
C_{00(\otimes)}
, is a curve appearing exclusively in tadpoles, and carries zero momentum.

We have enumerated all the curves and their associated momenta. One can construct explicit
g
-vector fans and the dual headlight functions. Though no simpler way other than analysing the curves on the doubled orientable surface is known.

Surface Symanzik for two-loop non-orientable surface:

We construct the spanning-1 and 2 subsurfaces of the
S_{(2)}
surface below, and consequently write down the surface Symanzik polynomials.

\displaystyle\text{Spanning-1 sub-surfaces of }\,S_{(2)}:

\displaystyle\left\{\hbox to58.45pt{\vbox to64.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.22495pt\lower-35.4863pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 27.56 0 C 27.56 15.22 15.22 27.56 0 27.56 C -15.22 27.56 -27.56 15.22 -27.56 0 C -27.56 -15.22 -15.22 -27.56 0 -27.56 C 15.22 -27.56 27.56 -15.22 27.56 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

(51)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\mathcal{U}_{S_{(2)}}=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i0}\left(\sum_{j\leq k}\alpha_{jk}^{\otimes}+\alpha_{0j}^{\otimes}+\alpha_{00}^{\otimes}\right)+\sum_{k}\alpha_{0k}^{\otimes}\left(\alpha_{00}^{\otimes}+\sum_{i\leq j}\alpha_{ij}^{\otimes}\right)~.

{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 23.87 C 17.91 9.65 0 -24.8 -13.78 -23.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 13.78 -23.87 C 17.91 -9.65 0 24.8 -13.78 23.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.375,0.25,0.375}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -11.02 0 L 27.56 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -11.02 0 M -8.27 0 C -8.27 1.52 -9.5 2.76 -11.02 2.76 C -12.55 2.76 -13.78 1.52 -13.78 0 C -13.78 -1.52 -12.55 -2.76 -11.02 -2.76 C -9.5 -2.76 -8.27 -1.52 -8.27 0 Z M -11.02 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right\}

(53)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\Rightarrow\quad\mathcal{F}_{S_{(2)}}=\sum_{i<{k-1}}X_{ik}\left[\alpha_{i0}\,\alpha_{k0}\left(\sum_{i\leq j\leq k}\alpha_{j0}^{\otimes}+\sum_{i\leq j\leq l\leq k}\alpha_{jl}^{\otimes}+\alpha_{00}^{\otimes}\right)+\alpha_{00}^{\otimes}\,\alpha_{i0}\,\alpha_{k0}^{\otimes}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\hskip 113.81102pt+\alpha^{\otimes}_{i0}\,\alpha_{k0}^{\otimes}\left(\alpha_{00}^{\otimes}+\sum_{k\leq j\leq i}\alpha_{j0}+\sum_{k\leq l\leq j\leq i}\alpha_{jl}^{\otimes}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\sum_{i\leq k\leq j\leq l}(P_{kj}+P_{li})^{2}\alpha_{kl}^{\otimes}\left[\alpha_{i0}\,\alpha_{j0}^{\otimes}+\alpha_{ij}^{\otimes}\left(\sum_{l\leq m\leq i}\alpha_{m0}+\sum_{k\leq m\leq j}\alpha_{m0}^{\otimes}\right)\right]~.

(54)

The third surface Symanzik polynomial
\mathcal{Z}
is given by the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{S_{(2)}}=\sum_{i<j-1}\alpha_{ij}X_{ij}~.

(55)

With this information, we can write down the scattering amplitude as follows:

\displaystyle A_{n}^{S_{(2)}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t_{0}\,\mathrm{d}t_{0}^{\prime}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}^{n+1}\vec{t}\ \mathcal{K}\,\frac{1}{\mathcal{U}^{d/2}}\,e^{-\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}-\mathcal{Z}}~.

(56)

6
Tropicalization of Möbius strip string amplitude

In this section, we revisit the field theory limit of the type-I superstring one-loop amplitudes with four massless particles. It is famously known that the field theory limit of type-I open strings gives the
N=4
super Yang-Mills amplitudes
Green:1982sw
;
Green:1987mn
. Our objective in this analysis is to see the combinatorial polytope
M_{n}
appearing out of the degeneration of the Möbius strip moduli space. We check that the field theory limit of the four-particle amplitude gives us the expected eight box diagrams from the polytope
M_{4}
with the appropriate momenta. This serves as a check for our curve integral construction, and for
P_{ij}^{\otimes}=l+P_{1i}+P_{1j}~.

6.1
Warmup: tropicalization of annulus string amplitude

Consider a complex half plane, parameterized by
\rho
. One can obtain an annulus by the following identification:
\rho\sim w\rho~.
Let us express
\rho=e^{2\pi i\zeta}~,
and we have
\zeta\sim\zeta+it~.
Figure
13
depicts the fundamental region under this identification. There are two boundaries:
\rho\in[w,1]
and
\rho\in[-w,-1]~.

Im
(\zeta)
Re
(\zeta)
\zeta
1/2
it
\frac{1}{2}+it
0
Re
(\rho)
Im
(\rho)
\rho=e^{2\pi i\zeta}
\rho\sim w\rho
1
-1
0
w
-w
=e^{-2\pi t}

Figure 13:
Annulus obtained by identifying
\rho
uppper half plane as
\rho\sim w{\rho}~.
The fundamental region under identification is shaded, and the thick lines mark the two distinct boundaries of the annulus.

Let us have four marked insertions on the boundary
[w,1]
with a fixed ordering. We use the translation on the boundary to fix
\zeta_{4}=it~:

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}\equiv it\nu_{i}~,\qquad\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}\leq\nu_{3}\leq\nu_{4}=1~.

(57)

Superstring amplitude:

The open string amplitude, due to annulus worldsheet for four massless external particles in type I superstring theory is as follows
9
9

9

Changing the integration variable to
1/w
in place of
w
, and using appropriate modular properties of
\theta_{1}
, one can rewrite the amplitude in a neater expression, as given in
Eberhardt:2023xck
.

Green:1987mn
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{annulus}}[1234]=N\,g_{s}^{4}\,\text{Tr}\big(t_{a_{1}}t_{a_{2}}t_{a_{3}}t_{a_{4}}\big)\,t_{8}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\left(\frac{-2\pi}{\log w}\right)^{5}\int\prod_{r=1}^{3}\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho_{r}}{\rho_{r}}\prod_{r<s}\big(\psi_{rs}\big)^{\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s}}~.

(58)

Note that
k_{i}^{2}=0
. The factor
t_{8}
contains the polarization vectors and kinematic factors corresponding to various scattering states in the supermultiplet. For
k_{i}^{2}=0
, the factor
\prod\psi
is given by the following:

\displaystyle\prod_{r<s}\big(\psi_{rs}\big)^{k_{r}.k_{s}}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,s=1\\
r<s\end{subarray}}^{4}e^{\pi(\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s})\zeta_{rs}^{2}/t}\ \theta_{1}\left(\zeta_{rs}\,\big|\,it\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s}}~,\qquad\quad\big(\zeta_{rs}=\zeta_{s}-\zeta_{r}\big)~.

(59)

We are interested in the moduli space integral, so let us drop the overall factor
N\,g_{s}^{4}\text{Tr}()
for now. Also, let us change the integration variables to
t
and
\zeta_{r}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Ann}}\equiv\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{5}}\,\int_{0}^{it}\prod_{r=1}^{3}\mathrm{d}\zeta_{r}\ \prod_{r<s}^{4}e^{\pi(\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s})\zeta_{rs}^{2}/t}\ \prod_{r<s}\theta_{1}\left(\zeta_{rs}\,\big|\,it\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s}}~.

(60)

We introduce a Gaussian integral over the loop momentum to facilitate the comparison with the QFT one-loop amplitude, as follows (
d=10
):

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\,e^{-\pi t\alpha^{\prime}\left(l^{2}-2\sum_{r}\nu_{r}\,l.k_{r}\right)}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\,e^{-\pi t\alpha^{\prime}\left(l-\sum_{r}\nu_{r}k_{r}\right)^{2}}\ e^{\pi t\alpha^{\prime}\left(\sum\nu_{r}k_{r}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t^{d/2}}\,e^{\pi\alpha^{\prime}\sum_{r<s}k_{r}.k_{s}\,\zeta_{rs}^{2}/t}~.

(61)

Plugging it back into the amplitude, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Ann.}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\int_{0}^{it}\prod_{r=1}^{3}\mathrm{d}\zeta_{r}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\,e^{-\pi t\alpha^{\prime}\left(l^{2}-2\sum_{r}\nu_{r}\,l.k_{r}\right)}\ \prod_{r<s}\theta_{1}\left(\zeta_{rs}\,\big|\,it\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s}}~.

(62)

Obtaining the box integral:

We have four external massless particles, and a loop momentum
l
. We have the following scalar kinematic variables, constructed out of
k_{i}
and
l
, motivated by the triangulations of an annulus:

\displaystyle X_{13}=(k_{1}+k_{2})^{2}~,\qquad X_{24}=(k_{2}+k_{3})^{2}~,\qquad X_{0i}=(l+k_{1}+\ldots+k_{i-1})^{2}~.

(63)

One can express
l^{2}
and
2l.k_{i}
in terms of these as follows:

\displaystyle l^{2}=X_{10}~,\qquad 2l.p_{1}

\displaystyle=X_{20}-X_{10}~,\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle 2l.p_{2}

\displaystyle=X_{30}-X_{20}-X_{13}~,

(64)

\displaystyle 2l.p_{3}

\displaystyle=X_{40}-X_{30}+X_{13}~,\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad 2l.p_{4}

\displaystyle=X_{10}-X_{40}~.

We have,

\displaystyle l^{2}-2\sum_{r}\nu_{r}l.k_{r}=X_{10}(\nu_{1})+X_{20}(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})+X_{30}(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})+X_{40}(1-\nu_{3})+X_{13}(\nu_{2}-\nu_{3})

Let us change to the following
\eta_{I}
variables.
I
refers to the chords/propagators in the triangulations
\{X_{10},~X_{20},~X_{30},~X_{40}\}~.

\displaystyle\hbox to164.68pt{\vbox to123.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.55826pt\lower-20.11684pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 42.67912pt\begin{matrix}\eta_{10}=t(\nu_{1})\\
\eta_{20}=t(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})\\
\eta_{30}=t(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})\\
\eta_{40}=t(1-\nu_{3})\end{matrix}~,\qquad\sum_{I\in T}\eta_{I}=t~.

(65)

The string amplitude (
62
) expressed in terms of the kinematic variables
X_{I}
and the worldsheet coordinates
\eta_{I}
, is as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Ann}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\mathrm{d}\eta_{i0}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\ e^{-\pi\alpha^{\prime}\sum_{I}\eta_{I}X_{I}}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times\left(\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{12})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{34})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\,e^{2\pi t(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}X_{13}/2}\left(\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{23})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{14})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}X_{24}/2}~.

(66)

We take the tropical limit of this integral as follows. Consider the high string tension limit
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0~,
but scaling the worldsheet coordinates such that the whole worldsheet does not collapse to a point:
\eta_{I}\to\eta_{I}/\alpha^{\prime}~.
In this limit, the worldsheet degenerates into a worldline, and the worldsheet coordinates
\eta_{I}
become the Schwinger parameters. With the limit
t\to t/\alpha^{\prime}~,
one can check the following using the infinite product expansion of
\theta_{1}

10
10

10

The Jacobi theta function
\theta_{1}
has the following product representation:

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(\zeta|\tau)=2e^{\pi i\tau/4}\,\sin(\pi\zeta)\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-e^{2\pi im\tau})(1-e^{2\pi i(m\tau+\zeta)})(1-e^{2\pi i(m\tau-\zeta)})~.

(67)

In the limit
\tau\to i\infty
, and
\zeta
not compensating for it, the infinite product can be replaced by 1, and we have:

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{12})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{34})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\xrightarrow{\ \ \tau\to i\infty\ \ }\frac{\sin(\pi\zeta_{12})\,\sin(\pi\zeta_{34})}{\sin(\pi\zeta_{13})\,\sin(\pi\zeta_{24})}\to e^{\pi t(\nu_{12}+\nu_{34}-\nu_{13}-\nu_{24})}=e^{2\pi t(\nu_{2}-\nu_{3})}~.

(68)

:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{12})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{34})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\,e^{2\pi t(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})}\right)~,~~\left(\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{23})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{14})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\right)\xrightarrow{\ \ \ t\to\infty\ \ \ \ }1~.

(69)

With all the
\eta_{I}
large, one can convince oneself that the worldsheet has degenerated into a box diagram with four propagators:
X_{i0}~,
and the amplitude becomes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Ann}}\to\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\mathrm{d}\eta_{i0}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\ e^{-\pi\sum_{I}\eta_{I}X_{I}}~.

(70)

The worldsheet coordinates
\eta_{I}
have become the Schwinger parameters for the Feynman diagram. This box diagram corresponds to one of the vertices in the
\hat{D}_{4}
combinatorial polytope. The triangles and bubbles in the combinatorial polytope correspond to the region in the moduli space where certain
\eta_{I}\to 0~.
The moduli space allows for triangles, bubbles, and tadpoles, though the field theory limit of superstrings is special, and famously there are no triangles or bubbles in the one-loop amplitude of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. The above analysis is sufficient for us to study the field theory limit of Möbius strip string amplitude, and we revisit the triangles and bubbles later in more detail.

6.2
Field theory limit of Möbius string

The open string amplitude with Möbius strip topology is an integral over the moduli space of the Möbius strip with
n
marked points on its boundary. We argue that in the high string tension limit, the moduli space degenerates into the combinatorial polytope
M_{n}
, and the string amplitude, i.e., integration over the worldsheet moduli space, turns into the curve integral formula discussed earlier. For concreteness, we discuss a four-particle amplitude. Analysis for arbitrary
n
follows in the same manner.

Constructing and parameterizing a Möbius strip:

Consider a complex half plane, parameterized by
\rho
. We obtain a Möbius strip by identifying the complex plane as follows:
\rho\sim-\overline{\rho}\,w~.
The fundamental region under this identification is depicted in Figure
14
. Let us express
\rho
as
\rho=e^{2\pi i\zeta}
. The identification for
\zeta
is as follows:

\displaystyle\rho\sim-w\,\overline{\rho}~,\qquad\quad\zeta\sim\frac{1}{2}+it-\overline{\zeta}~.

(71)

The factor
w=e^{-2\pi t}
parameterizes the thickness of the Möbius strip. In the
\rho
plane, the boundary of the Möbius strip is
[w,1]\cup[-1,-w]~.
The single boundary appears divided into two parts in this parameterization. Hence, it is convenient to parameterize the boundary equivalently as
\rho\in[w^{2},1]
or
\zeta\in[0,2it]~.
The region
\rho\in[w^{2},w]
is equivalent to
\rho\in[-w,-1]
under the identification
\rho\sim-w\overline{\rho}~.
With a given color ordering, the marked insertions will lie on this boundary in the following order, and we use the translation on the boundary to fix
\zeta_{n}=2it~:

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}\equiv it\nu_{i}~,\quad\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\nu_{n}=2~.

(72)

(\zeta)
Re
(\zeta)
\zeta
1/2
\sim it

\sim 0

Figure 14:
Möbius strip obtained by identifying
\rho
uppper half plane as
\rho\sim-w\overline{\rho}~.
The fundamental region under identification is shaded, and the thick line marks the single boundary of the Möbius strip.

Superstring amplitude:

The open string amplitude, due to Möbius strip worldsheet, for massless external particles in type I superstring theory is given by the following
Green:1987mn
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Mob}}\big[1234\big]=g_{s}^{4}\,\text{Tr}\big(t_{a_{1}}t_{a_{2}}t_{a_{3}}t_{a_{4}}\big)\,t_{8}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\left(\frac{-2\pi}{\log w}\right)^{5}\int\prod_{r=1}^{3}\frac{\mathrm{d}\rho_{r}}{\rho_{r}}\prod_{r<s}\big(\psi^{N}_{rs}\big)^{\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s}}~.

(73)

Note that
k_{i}^{2}=0
. For
k_{i}^{2}=0
, the factor
\prod\psi^{N}
is given by the following:

\displaystyle\prod_{r<s}\big(\psi^{N}_{rs}\big)^{k_{r}.k_{s}}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,s=1\\
r<s\end{subarray}}^{4}e^{\pi(\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s})\zeta_{rs}^{2}/t}\theta_{1}\left(\zeta_{rs}\,\bigg|\,\frac{1}{2}+it\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s}}~,\qquad\quad\big(\zeta_{rs}=\zeta_{s}-\zeta_{r}\big)~.

(74)

Analogous to the annulus case, let us drop the prefactors
g_{s}^{4}\,\text{Tr}(t_{a_{1}}\ldots)\,t_{8}
, change the integration variables to
t
and
\zeta_{r}
, and introduce a gaussian loop integral (
61
), to obtain the following:
Plugging it back into the amplitude, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Mob}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\int_{0}^{2it}\prod_{r=1}^{3}\mathrm{d}\zeta_{r}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\,e^{-\pi t\alpha^{\prime}\left(l^{2}-2\sum_{r}\nu_{r}\,l.k_{r}\right)}\ \prod_{r<s}\theta_{1}\left(\zeta_{rs}\,\bigg|\,\frac{1}{2}+it\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}\,k_{r}.k_{s}}~.

(75)

Obtaining box diagrams:

We had constructed the invariants out of loop momenta
X_{ik}^{\otimes}=(l+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{i-1})+(p_{1}+\ldots+p_{k-1}))^{2}
while studying the curve integral formula. Let us trade off the
l^{2}~,2l.k_{r}
with
X_{ik}^{\otimes}~.
However, not all
X_{ik}^{\otimes}
are independent, and we have to choose a set of linearly independent
X_{ik}^{\otimes}
’s. Let us choose the following four, to begin with:
X_{11}^{\otimes}~,X_{12}^{\otimes}~,X_{13}^{\otimes}~,X_{14}^{\otimes}~.
We have the following identity:

\displaystyle l^{2}-2\sum_{r}\nu_{r}\,l.k_{r}=(\nu_{1}-1)X_{11}^{\otimes}+(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})X_{12}^{\otimes}+(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})X_{13}^{\otimes}+(2-\nu_{3})X_{14}^{\otimes}-(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})X_{13}~.

We express (
75
) in terms of
X_{ik}^{\otimes}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Mob}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,\int_{0}^{2it}\prod_{r=1}^{3}\mathrm{d}\zeta_{r}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\,e^{-\pi t\alpha^{\prime}\left\{(\nu_{1}-1)X_{11}^{\otimes}+(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})X_{12}^{\otimes}+(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})X_{13}^{\otimes}+(2-\nu_{3})X_{14}^{\otimes}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times\left(\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{12})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{34})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\,e^{2\pi t(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}X_{13}/2}\left(\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{23})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{14})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}X_{24}/2}~.

(76)

Let us restrict ourselves to a particular region in the moduli space with
\nu_{1}>1~,
so that we have the following configuration of the insertions on the boundary:

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 56.9055pt\begin{matrix}\eta_{14}=t(2-\nu_{3})\\
\eta_{13}=t(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})\\
\eta_{12}=t(\nu_{2}-\nu_{1})\\
\eta_{11}=t(\nu_{1}-1)\end{matrix}~,\qquad\sum_{I\in T}\eta_{I}=t~.

(77)

The newly introduced variables
\eta_{I}\in[0,t]~,
and add up to
t~.
The index
I
runs over
{(11),(12),(13),(14)}
, the chords in the particular triangulation
(T)
chosen. The string integral written in terms of these variables is as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Mob}}

\displaystyle\supset\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{I\in T}\mathrm{d}\eta_{I}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\,e^{-\pi\alpha^{\prime}\sum_{I}(\eta_{I}X_{I}^{\otimes})}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times\left(\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{12})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{34})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\,e^{2\pi t(\nu_{3}-\nu_{2})}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}X_{13}/2}\left(\frac{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{23})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{14})}{\theta_{1}(\zeta_{13})\,\theta_{1}(\zeta_{24})}\right)^{\alpha^{\prime}X_{24}/2}~.

(78)

We wish to take the tropical limit of this integral. It is exactly the same as the annulus case (
66
). We proceed in the same manner:
\eta_{I}\to\eta_{I}/\alpha^{\prime}
, and the factors with
X_{13}
and
X_{24}
go to 1 (
69
). So, in the tropical limit of the region of the moduli space given by (
77
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\text{Mob}}\supset\int_{0}^{\infty}\prod_{I\in T}\mathrm{d}\eta_{I}\int\mathrm{d}^{d}l\ e^{-\pi\sum_{I}(\eta_{I}X_{I}^{\otimes})}~.

(79)

We have obtained the box diagram dual to complete triangulation
\{X_{11}^{\otimes}\,,\ X_{12}^{\otimes}\,,\ X_{13}^{\otimes}\,,\ X_{14}^{\otimes}\}~.

In the above analysis, the marked points on the boundary of the Möbius strip were arranged such that
\zeta_{1}
and
\zeta_{4}
were far separated from each other, compared to the other pairs. With this, we argue that the worldsheet has degenerated into the following Feynman diagram:
